## Page 1


TORSION TRAJECTORIES FROM LOCAL DISCRIMINANTS TO
GLOBAL OBSTRUCTIONS
ABDUL RAHMAN
Abstract. For a normal surface singularity, the discrepancy between the ordinary and dual
middle-perversity intersection complexes over Z is measured by a finite group E. In previous
work, E was identified with link torsion, the exceptional-lattice discriminant group Λ∨/Λ,
a resolution-neighborhood boundary quotient, and, in the hypersurface case, coker(T −
id)tors. This paper tracks the trajectory of this torsion from local singularity data to global
obstruction theory. We follow the discriminant package (E, q) through support cohomology,
excision, global torsion, Brauer comparison, Bloch–Ogus residues, and rationalization. The
method is example-driven: trajectory tables are computed for A1, Ak, D4, E8, a non-
ADE Brieskorn singularity, the threefold ordinary double point, nodal threefolds, nodal
quintics, and the Benoist–Ottem benchmark. The computations reveal a sharp distinction:
a surface A1 singularity has local Z/2-torsion, whereas a threefold ordinary double point
has torsion-free link S2 × S3 and contributes free vanishing-cycle data instead. Thus finite
discriminant torsion is naturally a codimension-two phenomenon, not a generic feature of
nodes. The resulting pattern motivates a specialization problem: whether the Enriques 2-
torsion in Benoist–Ottem integral Hodge counterexamples is genuinely global, or can arise
after degeneration from transverse A1-type discriminant data along codimension-two strata.

1. Introduction
Intersection homology, perverse sheaves, and Hodge-theoretic decomposition theorems de-
pend essentially on the coefficient category.
The middle-perversity intersection complex
was introduced in the topological theory of Goresky–MacPherson and reformulated sheaf-
theoretically in the theory of perverse sheaves [1, 2]. With field coefficients, Verdier duality
preserves the middle-perversity t-structure, and for projective morphisms the decomposition
theorem expresses the derived direct image of an intersection complex as a direct sum of
shifted semisimple perverse sheaves [2, 3, 4, 5]. Over Z, the same formalism is sensitive to
torsion. The ordinary middle-perversity and dual middle-perversity t-structures need not
agree, because duality changes the torsion condition in the critical stalk or costalk degree.
The resulting discrepancy is not a defect of the theory; it is integral geometric data. It is al-
ready visible in Goresky–Siegel duality and in the ordinary/dual middle-perversity formalism
of BBD [6, 2].
The point of this paper is to treat these corrections as geometric data rather than as
technical noise. Integral torsion appears in several related forms: as torsion in local inter-
section homology, as linking pairings on singular links, as discriminant groups of exceptional
lattices, as cokernels of integral variation maps, as Brauer classes, and as unramified co-
homology classes [6, 7, 8, 9, 10, 11, 12]. These appearances are often studied in different
languages. The purpose of the present paper is to begin a local-to-global comparison among
them.
We study these integral and torsion-theoretic invariants as a diagnostic layer that records
precisely where integral structures fail to be seen by the rational theory. This viewpoint
is consistent with the role of torsion in integral intersection homology and torsion-sensitive
Deligne sheaves [7, 13, 8], and also with the role of torsion in known failures of the integral
Hodge conjecture [14, 15, 16, 12, 17]. Understanding how torsion is created locally, trans-
ported through resolutions, and either survives or dies globally is therefore a natural step
toward a more complete obstruction theory for integral Hodge phenomena.
The guiding point is that rationalization kills torsion but does not erase the geometric
mechanisms that produced it. A torsion class may vanish after tensoring with Q, yet before
this disappearance it may record a linking form, a lattice discriminant, a monodromy coker-
nel, a support relation, a Brauer class, or a closed-stratum gluing term in the MacPherson–
Vilonen description of perverse sheaves. Thus the central question is not whether torsion
survives rationalization, but what torsion forces before rationalization kills it.
1.1. The invariant E. The local input for this paper is the finite group E attached to a
normal surface singularity in [18]. Let (X, 0) be a germ of a normal complex analytic surface,
and let
j : U := X \ {0} ,→X

be the inclusion of the punctured germ. The ordinary and dual middle-perversity intersection
complexes with integral coefficients are denoted
pICXZ,
p
+ICXZ.
Both restrict to the shifted constant sheaf ZU[2] on U, but they need not agree over Z.
Following Jung–Saito and [18], define
E := H0(p
+ICXZ)0.
Equivalently, E is the finite group appearing in the distinguished triangle
pICXZ −→p
+ICXZ −→E[1] −→.
Here E is viewed as a complex supported at the singular point. Thus E measures the local
integral discrepancy between the ordinary and dual middle extensions [19, 18].
This discrepancy is invisible rationally. After tensoring with Q, the ordinary and dual
middle-perversity intersection complexes become canonically identified:
pICXQ ∼= p
+ICXQ.
Consequently E is a purely integral invariant. It records the torsion correction that survives
over Z but disappears after rationalization, precisely the phenomenon that the integral and
torsion-sensitive theories are designed to detect [2, 7, 18].
1.2. Six local realizations. The main local input for this paper is the finite group E
attached to a normal surface singularity in [18]. Let L be the link of the singularity, let
π : e
X →X be the minimal resolution, let Λ be the exceptional lattice generated by the
irreducible exceptional curves, and let M be the corresponding intersection matrix. The
main theorem of [18] identifies E through four broad realizations:
perverse discrepancy,
H2(L, Z)tors,
Λ∨/Λ,
coker(T −id)tors,
where the last realization is available in the isolated hypersurface case. More explicitly,
E ∼= H2(L, Z)tors ∼= Λ∨/Λ,
and hence
|E| = | det(M)|.
If (X, 0) is an isolated hypersurface surface singularity and T denotes the Milnor monodromy
on integral vanishing cohomology, then
E ∼= coker(T −id)tors.
Under the additional hypothesis that
(T −id) ⊗Z Q
is an isomorphism, this gives
|E| = | det(T −id)|.
The link-topological, resolution-theoretic, and monodromy-theoretic inputs used in these
identifications are classical; see, for example, [20, 10, 6, 9]. The precise compatibility of
these realizations in the present notation is established in [18].
For the purposes of the torsion-trajectory program, we refine these four broad realizations
into six operational interfaces. The reason for this refinement is that local-to-global transport
requires not only knowing the finite group E, but also knowing which geometric operation
exposes it. The perverse realization separates into the cone description and the Friedman

torsion-sensitive truncation interpretation.
The topological and resolution-theoretic real-
izations separate into link cohomology, the exceptional-lattice discriminant group, and the
resolution-neighborhood pair-sequence boundary quotient. The hypersurface case adds the
Wang-sequence or monodromy realization. Thus the same finite group is visible through the
following six operational realizations:
(1) The perverse cone
Cone (pICXZ →p
+ICXZ) ,
where E appears as the point-supported correction measuring the difference between
the ordinary and dual middle-perversity intersection complexes [18, Theorem 1.1];
(2) The Friedman torsion-sensitive truncation discrepancy between the ordinary and dual
middle-perversity packages, which gives the torsion-sensitive Deligne-sheaf interpre-
tation of the same integral correction term [7, 13] and is compared with the ordi-
nary/dual BBD perversity conventions in [18, Appendix C];
(3) The link cohomology group
H2(L, Z)tors,
where L is the link of the normal surface singularity; this is the local-topological
realization of E [18, Theorem 3.8], and it is compatible with the classical linking-
duality picture for singularity links [6];
(4) The exceptional-lattice discriminant group
Λ∨/Λ,
where Λ is the lattice generated by the exceptional curves of the minimal resolution;
this is the resolution-theoretic realization [18, Theorem 4.7], with the discriminant-
form formalism following the standard lattice conventions of Nikulin [9];
(5) The boundary quotient appearing in the long exact sequence of the resolution - neigh-
borhood pair (N, L), where N is a sufficiently small resolution neighborhood of the
exceptional divisor and L = ∂N. Under Poincaré–Lefschetz duality, the map
H2(N, Z) →H2(N, L; Z)
is identified with the lattice map
Λ →Λ∨,
and its finite cokernel is Λ∨/Λ [18, Lemmas 4.3–4.5];
(6) and, in the isolated hypersurface case, the Wang-sequence or Milnor-monodromy
realization
coker(T −id)tors,
where T is the integral Milnor monodromy on vanishing cohomology [18, Theorem 1.3
and Section 5]; the underlying Milnor-fibration and monodromy formalism is classical
[10].
These six operational realizations are not merely redundant computations. They provide
different interfaces with the geometry. The perverse cone connects E to integral middle
perversities [2, 19]. The torsion-sensitive truncation realization connects it to Friedman’s
torsion-sensitive Deligne sheaves [7, 13]. The link realization connects it to local topology
and linking pairings [6]. The lattice realization equips it with a discriminant form
q : E × E −→Q/Z,


## Page 10


10
ABDUL RAHMAN
which can be compared with the torsion linking pairing on the link [6, 9]. The pair-sequence
realization is the natural entry point for local-to-global transport through a resolution. The
monodromy realization connects the same group to Picard–Lefschetz theory and the integral
variation map T −id [10].
The present paper asks which of these realizations is best suited for global transport.
More precisely, if X is a projective variety with isolated singularities p1, . . . , ps, and if each
singularity carries a local discriminant package (Ei, qi), we ask when the direct sum of these
local packages maps to global torsion invariants of a resolution.
The later appendices make this transport language explicit. Appendix L fixes the realiza-
tion notation used for Betti, étale, Hodge, Nori, Brauer, unramified, and stacky comparison
stations. Appendix M records the six-operation transport dictionary used to compare local,
smooth, stacky, and motivic torsion packages. These appendices are not used to reprove
the cited six-functor formalisms; they specify how those formalisms organize the torsion-
trajectory program.
1.3. The global targets. Let
π : e
X −→X
be a resolution of a projective variety with isolated singularities. The global torsion targets
considered in this paper are
H∗( e
X, Z)tors,
Br( e
X),
H3
nr( e
X, Q/Z).
The first target is ordinary integral cohomological torsion. The second is the cohomological
Brauer group. The third is unramified cohomology, defined by residue conditions in the
Bloch–Ogus formalism [11, 12].
For smooth projective threefolds, degree-four integral Hodge questions are closely tied
to torsion phenomena in degree three. The exponential and Kummer sequences relate the
Brauer group to H3( e
X, Z)tors under suitable hypotheses, while the Bloch–Ogus formalism
detects unramified cohomology through the vanishing of codimension-one residues [11, 12].
These groups appear naturally in the study of failures and refinements of the integral Hodge
conjecture [14, 15, 16, 21, 12, 17, 22]. Thus, in the threefold setting, the relevant global
torsion target is not an unspecified cohomology group but specifically
H3( e
X, Z)tors.

The third bridge, γX, is governed by Bloch–Ogus residues. This bridge is different from
the Brauer comparison above. The Brauer bridge is an identification, under suitable hy-
potheses, between degree-three integral torsion and the cohomological Brauer group. The
unramified bridge is instead a survival test: it asks whether a class has zero residue at every
codimension-one point. This residue-theoretic description is the basic Bloch–Ogus interpre-
tation of unramified cohomology [11, 12].
Let Y be a smooth projective complex variety. In the Bloch–Ogus formalism, unramified

unramified station in integral Hodge obstruction theory is precisely the mechanism used by
Colliot-Thélène–Voisin in their comparison between degree-four integral Hodge phenomena
and degree-three unramified cohomology [12].

1.6. Main results. The first part of the paper recalls the local discriminant package and
relates it to divisor-theoretic invariants. Under the standard resolution-lattice hypotheses for
normal surface singularities, the local class group is identified with the discriminant group:
Cl(OX,0) ∼= Λ∨/Λ ∼= E.
This comparison connects the perverse-sheaf invariant E with the factoriality and Q-factoriality
questions that motivate the Jung–Saito framework [19, 18].
The second part constructs the degree-indexed local-to-global maps.

especially D4, to show why the pairing must be tracked. The general compatibility of the
local-to-global map with global torsion pairings is formulated as a problem. This part uses

1.7. Main contributions. The paper makes six contributions. First, it organizes the local
invariant E into a torsion trajectory, tracking birth, discriminant form, local realizations,
support transport, Brauer comparison, residue behavior, and rationalization.
Second, it
computes this trajectory in a uniform format for surface ADE, non-ADE, quotient, threefold-
node, nodal, and Benoist–Ottem/Coble benchmark examples. Third, it separates local finite
discriminant torsion from free vanishing-cycle relations and from global smooth torsion.
In particular, a surface A1 singularity contributes local Z/2-torsion, whereas a threefold
ordinary double point has torsion-free link S2 × S3. Fourth, it formulates the codimension-
two transverse torsion principle using MacPherson–Vilonen gluing: transverse surface torsion
appears as a closed-stratum perverse discrepancy term. Fifth, it corrects the Benoist–Ottem
comparison by showing that the relevant Enriques/Coble boundary singularity is 1
4(1, 1),
with E ∼= Z/4, and that the Benoist–Ottem 2-torsion mechanism selects the order-two
Bockstein shadow 2E ∼= Z/2. Sixth, it records a motivic lift of this structure: the full Coble
package is modeled by i∗(1Σ/4)[2], while the Benoist–Ottem order-two shadow is modeled
by i∗(1Σ/2)[2].
1.8. Organization of the paper. Section 2 introduces the torsion trajectory and the main
tables. It defines the stations through which local torsion is tracked: birth, form, local real-
ization, excision, global survival, Brauer comparison, residue behavior, and rationalization.
Section 3 fixes conventions on degrees, signs, discriminant forms, and torsion-sensitive trun-
cations. It also records the degree bookkeeping needed for isolated singularities of different
dimensions. Section 4 recalls the local discriminant package E, its compatible realizations,
and its comparison with local class groups in the surface case. Section 5 constructs the
degree-indexed local-to-global maps α(k)
X using excision and cohomology with supports. It
also formulates kernel criteria in terms of global relations among exceptional or support
classes. Section 6 treats the Brauer comparison through the exponential and Kummer se-
quences. Section 7 treats unramified cohomology and gives the residue survival criterion.
Section 8 extracts the patterns observed in the trajectory computations: non-unimodularity
as the source of local finite torsion, E8 as the null-control case, the form-sensitivity of D4,
the distinction between surface A1 torsion and threefold ordinary double point free data,
the codimension-two nature of transverse surface torsion, and the corrected Coble boundary
interpretation of the Benoist–Ottem comparison. Section 9 states the resulting trajectory
principles, functorial constructions, and open problems, including the codimension-two trans-
verse torsion principle, the MacPherson–Vilonen closed-stratum gluing interpretation, the
motivic lift i∗(1Σ/n)[2], and the Benoist–Ottem/Coble order-two shadow theorem.
The appendices contain the detailed computations. They work out the A1, Ak, D4, E8,
non-ADE Brieskorn, Coble 1
4(1, 1), threefold ordinary double point, nodal threefold, Benoist–
Ottem, and nodal quintic trajectories, followed by a reference appendix collecting the exact
sequences used throughout. Appendix L fixes realization notation for motivic torsion pack-
ages. Appendix M records the six-operation transport dictionary used to compare torsion

1.9. How to read this paper. This paper is deliberately modular. The main text gives the
conceptual trajectory: local birth, discriminant form, transport, Brauer comparison, residue
survival, and rational death. The appendices give the computations that justify the rows of
the tables. The reader need not read the appendices linearly on a first pass. Instead, the
tables in Section 2 can be used as a guide: each row points to a corresponding appendix
where the claim is verified.
For a first reading, we recommend Sections 1–2 for the overview, Sections 4–7 for the
main local-to-global mechanisms, Section 9 for the Benoist–Ottem/Coble comparison, and
Appendices L–M for the realization and six-operation dictionary. The remaining appendices
may be consulted as needed. This organization reflects the purpose of the paper: to make
the torsion trajectory both conceptually visible and computationally checkable.
2. The torsion trajectory and the main tables
The constructions in this paper are organized around a single question: what happens to
integral torsion as it moves from local singularity data to global cohomology, then to Brauer
and unramified cohomology, and finally disappears after rationalization?
The local input is the finite group
E = H0(p
+ICXZ)0
attached to a normal surface singularity. In [18], this group was computed through compat-
ible realizations:
E ∼= H2(L, Z)tors ∼= Λ∨/Λ,
and, in the isolated hypersurface case,
E ∼= coker(T −id)tors.
Here L is the link of the singularity, Λ is the exceptional lattice of a resolution, and T is
the Milnor monodromy. These identifications are not merely different calculations of the
same group. They show that the same torsion phenomenon is visible in perverse sheaves,
torsion-sensitive truncation, link topology, exceptional lattices, resolution-neighborhood pair
sequences, and monodromy.
The purpose of the present paper is to track this torsion as a trajectory. At each station
we ask whether the class exists, what structure it carries, whether it survives the next
exact sequence, and what kills it if it does not survive.
The tables below are designed
to keep five mechanisms distinct: local finite discriminant torsion, free vanishing-cycle data,
global smooth torsion, Brauer/unramified obstruction, and Bockstein-selected torsion shadows
inside larger local E-packages. Tables 1, 2, 3, and 4 summarize the notation, local birth data,
local realization data, and transport behavior used throughout the paper.
2.1. The stations of the trajectory. We organize the movement of torsion into seven
stations.
(1) Birth. In the surface singularity setting, finite torsion is born locally as Ei ∼= Λ∨
i /Λi.
At this station we record the group structure, invariant factors, order, and prime
decomposition. For example, A1 has E ∼= Z/2Z, Ak has E ∼= Z/(k + 1)Z, E8 has
E = 0, and the Coble boundary singularity 1
4(1, 1) has E ∼= Z/4Z.

linking pairing on the link; see the linking-pairing formalism of [6] and the lattice
discriminant formalism of [9]. The D4 row shows that the group alone is not always
enough, while the Coble boundary row shows that a global 2-torsion mechanism may
select only a subgroup of a larger local package:
2E ∼= Z/2 ⊂E ∼= Z/4.
(3) Local realization. The same finite group is computed through the available local
models: perverse discrepancy, torsion-sensitive truncation, link torsion, exceptional
lattice, pair sequence, and, in the hypersurface case, monodromy. The agreement
of these realizations in the surface case is the local theorem of [18]. In the tables,
the entry “same E” means that all of the available stations recover the same finite
group. For quotient singularities such as 1
4(1, 1), the monodromy station is not part
of the hypersurface Wang-sequence realization, but the perverse, torsion-sensitive,
link, lattice, and pair-sequence stations still recover the same E.

The point is not that torsion survives rationalization; it does not.
The point is
that before it dies, it records integral data invisible over Q. In the Coble boundary
example, both the full package
E ∼= Z/4
and the Benoist–Ottem order-two shadow
2E ∼= Z/2
die rationally, but they encode different integral information before they disappear.
2.2. Legend for trajectory tables. We use the following notation in the trajectory tables.
Table 1. Legend for the torsion trajectory tables.
Symbol
Meaning
✓
The class or package survives this station.
0
The class dies or the relevant group is zero.
?
The station requires a separate computation before any
global assertion can be made.
∅
The station is not defined in this example.
rel
The class is killed by a global relation.
res
The class is killed by a residue map.
free
The local data is free abelian, not finite torsion.
global
The torsion is global smooth torsion rather than local
singularity torsion.
shadow
A subgroup or filtered piece selected inside a larger local
E-package.
The symbol ? is important. It is not a placeholder for carelessness. It marks the exact point
at which a computation must be performed before the trajectory can continue. For example,
the ordinary double point in complex dimension three cannot be assigned the same row as
the A1 surface singularity without recomputing the link, support degree, and monodromy
station. That computation shows that the link is S2 × S3, hence torsion-free.

2.4. Six-realization table. The second table records which local realizations are available
and what they produce. The phrase “same E” means that the realization produces the finite
group listed in the previous table.
This table is the main evidence for the first organizing principle of the paper: for normal
surface singularities in the examples considered, finite torsion is stable across the available
local realizations. The threefold node row is included as a warning. It is not a counterexample
to the surface theorem; it is a different-dimensional local model. The Coble row is included
as the corrected Enriques boundary model: the available surface realizations recover the full
E ∼= Z/4, while the Benoist–Ottem mechanism detects only the Bockstein-selected order-two
subgroup.
2.5. Transport and obstruction table. The third table records what happens after the
local station. This is where global geometry enters.
The key separation in this table is between local finite torsion and other forms of topology.
The threefold ordinary double point has no finite local torsion, but it has free vanishing-cycle
data. Nodal defect concerns global relations among these free classes. Benoist–Ottem torsion
is global smooth torsion on a smooth variety, not direct local singularity torsion. The correct
boundary comparison is supplied by the Coble 1
4(1, 1) row: the full local E-package is Z/4,
while the Benoist–Ottem 2-torsion direction is the Bockstein-selected subgroup 2E ∼= Z/2.

2.6. Why the examples come early. The examples in this paper are not illustrations of
a completed abstract theorem. They are the data from which the global pattern is extracted.
The formal results below explain the columns of the table:
• the local discriminant package explains the birth and form columns;
• the six-realization theorem explains the local model columns;
• Mayer–Vietoris, pair sequences, excision, and cohomology with supports explain the
support and transport columns;
• Bockstein exact sequences explain how a mod-2 double-cover class can select an
order-two subgroup inside a larger local E-package;
• MacPherson–Vilonen and Beilinson gluing explain how transverse surface torsion
appears as a closed-stratum perverse discrepancy term;
• the exponential sequence explains the Brauer column;
• Bloch–Ogus residues explain the unramified survival column;
• rationalization explains the final disappearance of finite torsion over Q.
Thus the paper proceeds in two interlocking directions. The theorem sections prove that
the stations of the trajectory are well-defined under stated hypotheses.
The appendices
compute the trajectory row by row. The final sections extract the patterns that the rows
reveal.
2.7. The main empirical questions. The tables are designed to answer the following
questions.
Question 2.1. Does the survival of local finite torsion depend only on the finite group Ei, or
does it depend on the full discriminant package (Ei, qi)?
Question 2.2. Which local torsion classes are killed by the forget-support map
Hk
D( e
X, Z)tors −→Hk( e
X, Z)tors?
Equivalently, which classes die because of global relations among exceptional, support, or
divisor classes?
Question 2.3. When a local torsion class survives to
H3( e
X, Z)tors,
does it determine a Brauer class, and if so, do its residues vanish?
Question 2.4. When a global 2-torsion mechanism is compared with a local singular boundary
package, does it see the full local group E, or only a Bockstein-selected subgroup such as
2E ⊂E?
Question 2.5. For the Benoist–Ottem examples, is the relevant local comparison with the
full Coble boundary package
E ∼= Z/4,
or specifically with its order-two shadow
2E ∼= Z/2?


## Page 23


TORSION TRAJECTORIES FROM LOCAL DISCRIMINANTS TO GLOBAL OBSTRUCTIONS
23
These questions do not assume that every local torsion class survives. In fact, death at
any station is part of the computation. A class may fail to be born, may die by a global
relation, may fail to enter the Brauer channel, may be killed by a residue, may be seen only
through a subgroup or quotient, or may disappear under rationalization. The point of the
trajectory is to record where and why this happens.
2.8. How theorems are extracted from the tables. The later formal statements should
be read as abstractions of the table entries. For example, the definition of
α(k)
X
abstracts the excision column. The kernel criterion abstracts the global survival column. The
Brauer comparison abstracts the Brauer column. The residue criterion abstracts the unram-
ified survival column. The MacPherson–Vilonen gluing theorem abstracts the codimension-
two closed-stratum column. The Bockstein calculations abstract the order-two shadow col-
umn in the Coble boundary example.
This organization is deliberate. The examples are computed first, with all available local
realizations. The theorems then record the functorial mechanisms that explain the repeated
patterns. Finally, the conjectures and comparison problems state the patterns that remain
after the computations are assembled across the rows.
3. Conventions, degrees, and local packages
This section fixes the conventions used throughout the paper. The main points are the sign
convention for exceptional intersection matrices, the normalization of discriminant forms,
the degree bookkeeping for isolated singularities, the notation for ordinary and dual middle
perversity over Z, and the convention for subpackages such as 2E ⊂E. These conventions are
needed because the same local torsion group can appear in different cohomological degrees
depending on the dimension of the singularity, because discriminant forms may differ by
sign depending on whether one uses the geometric or positive-definite lattice convention,
and because the Benoist–Ottem comparison uses an order-two Bockstein shadow inside the
larger local group E of the Coble boundary singularity.
3.1. Sign convention for intersection matrices. Throughout the paper, exceptional
intersection matrices are taken in the geometric convention. Thus if
π : e
X −→X
is a resolution of a normal surface singularity and
Eπ =

is the exceptional divisor, the intersection matrix is
M = (Ca · Cb).
For rational double points this is the negative of the corresponding Cartan matrix.
In

curve C ∼= P1 with
(C, C) = −2,
so the intersection matrix is
[−2].

For an Ak singularity, the matrix is the negative Ak Cartan matrix. Likewise, the D- and

This is the geometric convention used below when computing the Coble boundary package

This convention is the one used in [18].
It is also the convention most natural from
the geometry of surface resolutions. Some lattice-theoretic references use the corresponding
positive definite root lattice instead. Passing between the two conventions changes the sign
of the bilinear form and therefore changes the displayed representative of the discriminant
pairing by an overall sign. Since the paper compares local discriminant pairings with link
pairings, this sign choice is fixed once and for all here.
Remark 3.1. All determinant statements are insensitive to this sign convention. For example,
if M is the negative Ak Cartan matrix, then
| det(M)| = k + 1.
For the Coble boundary singularity 1
4(1, 1), the matrix is
M = [−4],
and therefore
| det(M)| = 4.
However, formulas for the discriminant form
q : Λ∨/Λ × Λ∨/Λ −→Q/Z
do depend on the sign convention. In this paper the discriminant form is computed from the
geometric negative definite intersection form.
3.2. Discriminant-form convention. Let (Λ, ( , )) be a nondegenerate integral lattice.
Thus Λ is a free abelian group of finite rank and
( , ) : Λ × Λ −→Z
is a nondegenerate symmetric bilinear form. Its dual lattice is
Λ∨:= HomZ(Λ, Z) ∼= {x ∈Λ ⊗Z Q | (x, λ) ∈Z for all λ ∈Λ}.
The discriminant group is
AΛ := Λ∨/Λ.
Since the form is nondegenerate over Q, the quotient AΛ is a finite abelian group.

Definition 3.2 (Discriminant pairing). The discriminant pairing of Λ is the Q/Z-valued
symmetric bilinear form
qΛ : AΛ × AΛ −→Q/Z
defined by
qΛ(x + Λ, y + Λ) := (x, y) mod Z,
x, y ∈Λ∨.
This pairing is well-defined because replacing x or y by an element differing by a vector in
Λ changes (x, y) by an integer. It is nondegenerate because Λ ⊂Λ∨is a finite-index inclusion
induced by a nondegenerate form. Discriminant forms of integral lattices are classical; see,
for example, Nikulin’s work on integral symmetric bilinear forms [9].
For a normal surface singularity, the exceptional lattice of the minimal resolution gives a
discriminant group
Λ∨/Λ.
In the previous paper this group was identified with the local integral perverse obstruction
group E [18]. Thus E carries not only the structure of a finite abelian group, but also a
canonical local discriminant pairing
q : E × E −→Q/Z.
The same finite pairing can be seen topologically through the linking form on the tor-
sion homology of the link. For a closed oriented three-manifold L, the torsion subgroup of
H1(L, Z) carries a nondegenerate linking pairing
λL : H1(L, Z)tors × H1(L, Z)tors −→Q/Z.
For links of normal surface singularities, this pairing is identified, up to the sign convention
fixed above, with the discriminant pairing of the exceptional lattice [6, 9, 18].

3.3. Degree shifts for isolated singularities. We next fix the basic degree bookkeeping.
Let (X, 0) be an isolated complex analytic singularity of pure dimension n. Let
U := X \ {0},

be the natural inclusions.
Let L be the link of the singularity.
For a sufficiently small
representative of the germ, the punctured neighborhood U is homotopy equivalent to L.
Therefore the stalk of the derived pushforward Rj∗ZU[n] at the singular point is computed
by the cohomology of L, shifted by n.
Lemma 3.5 (Local stalk degree bookkeeping). Let (X, 0) be an isolated complex analytic
singularity of pure dimension n, let U = X \ {0}, and let L be the link. Then there is a
canonical isomorphism
i∗Rj∗ZU[n] ∼= RΓ(L, Z)[n].
Consequently,
Hm i∗Rj∗ZU[n]

for all m ∈Z.

 ∼= (Rm+nj∗ZU)0.

of the singular point. This punctured neighborhood deformation retracts onto the link L.
Hence
(Rm+nj∗ZU)0 ∼= Hm+n(L, Z),
which proves the assertion.
□

Thus

In particular, the degree-zero stalk sees H2(L, Z). The torsion subgroup of this group is
precisely the local obstruction group E in the surface case:
E ∼= H2(L, Z)tors

Remark 3.6. This degree shift is the main reason for distinguishing the surface-type local the-

two features: the transverse surface package is computed from H2 of the three-dimensional
transverse link, while its contribution to the threefold is shifted and transported through
closed-stratum gluing and support cohomology.

4. The local discriminant package
In this section we recall the local invariant that supplies the input for the global construc-
tions in the rest of the paper. The invariant is the finite group E attached to a normal surface
singularity. It measures the difference between the ordinary and dual middle-perversity inter-
section complexes over Z. The definition and the local distinguished triangle are due to the
integral middle-perversity framework of BBD and the local surface analysis of Jung–Saito;
the compatible topological, resolution-theoretic, and monodromy realizations used here are
those proved in [18].
The corrected Benoist–Ottem comparison later in the paper uses this same local group E,
not a new invariant. In the Enriques/Coble boundary example, the relevant singularity is
the cyclic quotient singularity 1
4(1, 1). For that singularity,
E ∼= Z/4,
and the Benoist–Ottem 2-torsion mechanism selects the canonical order-two subgroup
2E ∼= Z/2.
Thus the Coble boundary example shows that a global 2-torsion mechanism may see a proper
Bockstein-selected shadow of a larger local E-package.
4.1. The perverse obstruction group E. Let (X, 0) be a germ of a normal complex
analytic surface. After shrinking X, we assume that 0 is the unique singular point. Put
U := X \ {0},
and let
j : U ,→X,
i : {0} ,→X
denote the inclusions. Since X is normal of complex dimension 2, the punctured germ U is
smooth.
We recall the precise convention for the intersection complexes used in this paper. For an
open inclusion j : U ,→X and a perverse sheaf F on U, the intermediate extension j!∗F is
defined in BBD as the image, in the perverse heart, of the natural morphism
pj!F −→pRj∗F.
Equivalently, j!∗F is the unique perverse extension of F to X with no nonzero subobject or
quotient object supported on the complement X \ U; see [2, §1.4]. In the integral setting
BBD also introduce the dual middle-perversity t-structure, which differs from the ordinary
middle perversity by the interchange of torsion and torsion-free conditions in the critical
degree; see [2, Complement 3.3].
With these conventions, the ordinary and dual middle-perversity intersection complexes
are defined by
pICXZ := pj!∗ZU[2],
p
+ICXZ := p
+j!∗ZU[2].
Here the shift by [2] is the standard normalization for a complex surface, so that ZU[2] is
perverse on the smooth complex surface U; see [2, §2.1] and the convention used in [18, §2.2].
By the defining property of intermediate extension, both complexes restrict to the object
from which they were extended:
j∗(pICXZ) ∼= ZU[2],
j∗(p
+ICXZ) ∼= ZU[2].

j∗(pICXZ) ∼= ZU[2] ∼= j∗(p

intersection matrix M is negative definite in the surface-resolution setting, by the classical
contraction criterion of Grauert; in this paper we use the same negative-definite geometric
convention as in [18, §4].
Theorem 4.5 (Local realization theorem). Let (X, 0) be a normal surface singularity with
link L, minimal resolution exceptional lattice Λ, and intersection matrix M. Then the local
perverse obstruction group satisfies
EX(0) ∼= H2(L, Z)tors ∼= Λ∨/Λ.
In particular,
|EX(0)| = | det(M)|.
If (X, 0) is an isolated hypersurface surface singularity and T is the Milnor monodromy on
integral vanishing cohomology, then
EX(0) ∼= coker(T −id)tors.

EX(0) is the finite point-supported correction between pICXZ and p
+ICXZ.
The topological realization
EX(0) ∼= H2(L, Z)tors
is proved in [18, Proposition 3.4]. The proof uses the local stalk computation
i∗Rj∗ZU[2] ∼= RΓ(L, Z)[2],
so that the degree-zero stalk sees H2(L, Z). The ordinary and dual middle-perversity con-
ditions then identify the discrepancy with the torsion subgroup of this group. The general
topological context for linking pairings and torsion phenomena in singular links is developed
in [6].
The resolution-lattice realization
H2(L, Z)tors ∼= Λ∨/Λ
is proved in [18, Theorem 1.2 and §4]. The argument uses the long exact sequence of the
pair (N, L), where N is a resolution neighborhood of the exceptional divisor and ∂N = L.
Poincaré–Lefschetz duality identifies the relevant relative homology group with the dual
lattice Λ∨. Under this identification, the map
H2(N, Z) −→H2(N, L; Z)
is represented by the intersection matrix M, equivalently by the lattice homomorphism
Λ −→Λ∨.
Therefore the finite quotient is
coker(Λ →Λ∨) = Λ∨/Λ.
Consequently
|EX(0)| = |Λ∨/Λ| = | det(M)|.
The discriminant-group formalism for integral lattices is standard; see [9].
Finally, assume that (X, 0) is an isolated hypersurface surface singularity. The Milnor
fibration identifies the link with the mapping torus of the Milnor fiber, and the Wang sequence
relates the cohomology of the link to the monodromy T. In the notation of this paper, this
gives
H2(L, Z)tors ∼= coker(T −id)tors.
This comparison is proved in [18, Theorem 1.3 and §5], using the standard Milnor-fibration
background from [10]. If
(T −id) ⊗Z Q
is an isomorphism, then coker(T −id) is finite. For an endomorphism of a free abelian group
whose rationalization is an isomorphism, the order of the finite cokernel is the absolute value
of the determinant. Hence
|EX(0)| = | det(T −id)|.
This determinant refinement is recorded in [18, Corollary 1.4].
□

For a general

The proposition is therefore stated only in the setting where the standard resolution-lattice
description of the local class group applies.
Remark 4.12. This comparison connects the perverse obstruction group E to the factoriality
and Q-factoriality questions motivating the Jung–Saito framework [19]. It also motivates the
later comparison with global Brauer groups. Local class groups measure local failure of fac-
toriality, while Brauer groups and unramified cohomology measure global torsion phenomena
that enter the integral Hodge problem [11, 12].

Let (Λ, ( , )) be the exceptional lattice. Its dual lattice is

Definition 4.13 (Local discriminant package). Let (X, 0) be a normal surface singularity
whose local obstruction group is identified with the exceptional discriminant group:
EX(0) ∼= Λ∨/Λ.
The local discriminant package is the pair
(E, q) := (Λ∨/Λ, qΛ),
where qΛ is the discriminant pairing induced by the exceptional intersection form.
The pairing qΛ is nondegenerate because the intersection form is nondegenerate over Q.
Via the link realization, this finite pairing agrees, up to the sign convention fixed in Section 2,
with the torsion linking pairing on the link. We record this as a proposition.
Proposition 4.14 (Discriminant and linking pairings). Let (X, 0) be a normal surface sin-
gularity for which the exceptional lattice realization
EX(0) ∼= Λ∨/Λ
is fixed. Let L be the link. Under the canonical identification
EX(0) ∼= H2(L, Z)tors ∼= Λ∨/Λ,
the discriminant pairing on Λ∨/Λ corresponds, up to the orientation sign convention, to the
torsion linking pairing of the link.
Proof. Let N be a resolution neighborhood of the exceptional divisor, with boundary L =
∂N. The long exact sequence of the pair (N, L) contains the segment
H2(N, Z) −→H2(N, L; Z) −→H1(L, Z) −→H1(N, Z).
Poincaré–Lefschetz duality identifies the relevant relative homology group with the dual
lattice Λ∨, and the map from H2(N, Z) to H2(N, L; Z) is represented by the exceptional
intersection form. Hence the finite quotient controlling the torsion in the boundary link is
Λ∨/Λ.
The linking pairing on H1(L, Z)tors is defined by taking a torsion cycle, multiplying it so
that it bounds, and measuring the resulting rational intersection number modulo Z. Under
the above identification of the boundary torsion with Λ∨/Λ, this construction is exactly the
inverse intersection-form construction of the discriminant pairing, up to the orientation sign
induced by the boundary convention. This is the standard comparison between link pairings
and discriminant forms [6, 9]. The compatibility with the local group EX(0) in the present
notation is part of the realization theorem proved in [18].
□

4.6. Why the form, not only the group, must be tracked. The local-to-global con-
structions below are formulated for packages
(Ei, qi),
not merely for the underlying finite groups Ei. The reason is that the same abstract finite
abelian group may carry inequivalent discriminant pairings. A construction that forgets the
pairing may therefore lose information visible both in the local topology and in the resolution
lattice.
The basic example is the contrast between a D4 rational double point and two independent
A1 rational double points. The D4 exceptional lattice has discriminant group
Λ∨
D4/ΛD4 ∼= Z/2Z ⊕Z/2Z.
Two independent A1 singularities also produce the underlying group
Z/2Z ⊕Z/2Z.
However, the discriminant pairings need not be the same. The distinction is invisible at the
level of groups but visible at the level of finite Q/Z-valued pairings. The general theory of
discriminant forms records precisely this extra information [9].
The Coble boundary example gives a second reason to keep the full package. There the
full local group is
E ∼= Z/4,
but the Benoist–Ottem 2-torsion mechanism sees only the subgroup
2E ∼= Z/2.
Thus even when a global comparison is 2-torsion, the local boundary package may be larger.
The correct statement is not that the global 2-torsion equals the full local group; rather, it
is that the global 2-torsion selects a Bockstein shadow inside the local package.
Remark 4.16. This is the reason the global transport problem is stated for
M

rather than for L

pairings map to natural global torsion pairings on the image in cohomology, in the Brauer
group, or in unramified cohomology. The ADE computations in the appendix provide the
first test cases for this form-sensitive version of the theory. The Coble computation adds a
filtered-subgroup test case, where the comparison with Benoist–Ottem uses 2E ⊂E rather
than the full local group.

The purpose of this section is to isolate the formal local-to-global mechanism used later
in the paper. The input is a projective variety X with isolated singularities and a resolution
π : e
X −→X.
The exceptional locus over each singular point has a small resolution neighborhood, and
cohomology with supports gives a natural way to compare local torsion data with global
cohomology. The key point is that this comparison is degree-dependent. We therefore define
degree-indexed maps
α(k)
X
rather than a single degree-free local-to-global map.
A second point will be used repeatedly. If a local torsion package contains a canonical
subgroup, then this subgroup carries its own local-to-global transport problem. In particular,
for the Coble boundary singularity 1
4(1, 1), the full local package is
E ∼= Z/4,
but the Benoist–Ottem 2-torsion mechanism selects the order-two subgroup
2E ∼= Z/2.
Thus in addition to transporting E, we must also be able to transport subpackages such as
2E ⊂E, together with the associated quotient sequence
0 −→2E −→E −→E/2E −→0.
We work throughout with singular cohomology with integral coefficients on the associated
analytic spaces. The formal properties used below are the standard long exact sequence
for cohomology with supports and the excision isomorphism; see, for example, Bredon’s
treatment of sheaf cohomology with supports and the usual relative-cohomology formulation
of excision [31, 32]. In the resolution-neighborhood calculations for surface singularities, the
relevant local pair sequence is the same one used in the comparison between link torsion and
exceptional discriminant groups in [6, 18].

explicit examples this subgroup may be described by defect groups, vanishing-cycle relations,
divisor-class relations, lattice embeddings, or Bockstein-selected subpackages such as
2E ⊂E.
5.9. Relation with Clemens defect. We conclude this section by explaining the expected
connection with nodal hypersurfaces. This part is included to orient the later examples; it
is not used as a theorem before the threefold node computation is completed.
For nodal hypersurfaces, the defect measures the failure of the nodes to impose independent
conditions, equivalently the presence of global relations among the local data associated to
the nodes. This circle of ideas goes back to Clemens’s work on double solids [25]. For explicit
treatments of the defect of nodal hypersurfaces, see Cynk [26]; for more recent families with
defect, see Kloosterman [27].
In the language of the present paper, the expected relation is that the kernel of the degree-
three local-to-global map for a nodal threefold should be governed by the same relation space
that appears in the defect.

The purpose of this section is to explain the second bridge in the local-to-global construc-

X, Z)tors.
For smooth projective complex varieties, the exponential sequence compares this group with
the cohomological Brauer group under standard vanishing hypotheses. This is the reason
the threefold degree-three target appears in the previous section.
We separate two points.
First, the analytic exponential sequence gives a comparison
between torsion in H2(Y, O∗

the torsion in analytic H2(Y, O∗

see Grothendieck’s Brauer papers and standard treatments of étale cohomology [33, 34].
A minor notational point will be useful later. The source of the degree-three map may be
a full local package Ei, or it may be a specified subpackage Ai ⊂Ei. In the Coble boundary

subpackage: it applies to whatever torsion subgroup has already survived into H3( e
X, Z)tors.

7. Unramified cohomology and residue survival
This section treats the residue station of the torsion trajectory. We state the definition of
unramified cohomology in the form used by Bloch–Ogus and Colliot-Thélène–Voisin, then
explain precisely what it means for a class coming from local discriminant data, or from a
specified subpackage such as 2E ⊂E, to survive to the unramified group.
There is one important degree issue. A class in H3(Y, Z)tors does not automatically become
a class in H3(Y, Q/Z) by the coefficient sequence
0 →Z →Q →Q/Z →0.
Rather, that sequence gives a Bockstein map
H2(Y, Q/Z) →H3(Y, Z),
and the torsion subgroup of H3(Y, Z) lies in the image of this Bockstein. Thus the residue
station below is formulated for a specified Q/Z-valued degree-three class associated to the
transported local data. In applications, producing such a class is part of the comparison
problem.
This warning is especially relevant for the Coble boundary comparison. The local boundary
package for the 1
4(1, 1) singularity is
E ∼= Z/4,
while the Benoist–Ottem 2-torsion mechanism selects
2E ∼= Z/2.
A residue calculation must be applied only after this order-two shadow, or the full local
package if appropriate, has been transported and converted into a specified Q/Z-valued
degree-three candidate.
7.1. Bloch–Ogus residues. Let Y be a smooth irreducible complex variety, and let K =
C(Y ) be its function field. For a codimension-one point D ∈Y (1), write k(D) for the residue
field of D. The point D defines a discrete valuation on K. In the Bloch–Ogus/Gersten
formalism, this valuation gives a residue homomorphism
∂D : Hr(K, Q/Z) −→Hr−1(k(D), Q/Z)
for the relevant étale cohomology groups with torsion coefficients. More precisely, in the
usual étale convention one includes Tate twists:
∂D : Hr(K, Q/Z(m)) −→Hr−1(k(D), Q/Z(m −1)).
Over the complex numbers, and for the purposes of this paper, we suppress Tate twists from
the notation. The residue formalism and its relation to the coniveau spectral sequence are
due to Bloch–Ogus [11]; see also the use of this formalism in the integral Hodge context by
Colliot-Thélène–Voisin [12].
The case used below is r = 3. Thus for each prime divisor D ∈Y (1) one has a residue map
∂D : H3(K, Q/Z) −→H2(k(D), Q/Z).
A class in H3(K, Q/Z) is called unramified if its residue at every codimension-one point
vanishes.

7.2. The coefficient sequence and the degree issue. We now explain why the residue
station must be formulated carefully. The short exact sequence of abelian groups
0 −→Z −→Q −→Q/Z −→0
gives a long exact sequence in cohomology:
· · · −→H2(Y, Q) −→H2(Y, Q/Z)
δ−→H3(Y, Z) −→H3(Y, Q) −→· · · .
Let
a ∈H3(Y, Z)tors.
Then the image of a in H3(Y, Q) is zero, because tensoring with Q kills torsion. By exactness,
a lies in the image of the Bockstein map
δ : H2(Y, Q/Z) −→H3(Y, Z).
Thus torsion in H3(Y, Z) is naturally detected by H2(Y, Q/Z) through the Bockstein, not
by an automatic map to H3(Y, Q/Z).
Remark 7.3. This is why the residue criterion below is stated for a specified class
ξ ∈H3(C(Y ), Q/Z)
associated to the transported local data.

nr(Y, Q/Z) is a
separate residue-theoretic target.

Remark 7.7. The theorem is formally simple because it is a survival criterion: once the
candidate class ξ exists, survival is exactly the vanishing of all codimension-one residues.
The difficult geometric work is usually the construction of ξ and the computation of its
residues.
7.4. Consequences for integral Hodge obstruction. We now explain how this residue
station relates to the integral Hodge problem. Let Y be a smooth projective complex variety.
The integral Hodge conjecture asks whether every integral Hodge class of degree 2p is the
cohomology class of an algebraic cycle of codimension p. It is false in general; classical and
modern counterexamples include [14, 15, 16, 17].
For degree-four classes, Colliot-Thélène and Voisin relate the failure of the integral Hodge
conjecture to unramified cohomology with Q/Z-coefficients [12]. Thus
H3
nr(Y, Q/Z)
is one of the natural obstruction groups appearing in the study of degree-four integral Hodge
classes.
Corollary 7.8. Let e ∈L
i Ei be a local discriminant class, or let a ∈L
i Ai be a class in
a specified subpackage. Suppose that the trajectory produces a residue candidate
ξ ∈H3(C( e
X), Q/Z).
If ξ is nonzero and all of its codimension-one residues vanish, then the corresponding trajec-
tory contributes a nonzero class to
H3
nr( e
X, Q/Z).
Consequently, it contributes to the same unramified cohomology group that appears in the
Colliot-Thélène–Voisin obstruction theory for degree-four integral Hodge classes.
Proof. If all residues of ξ vanish, then by the residue survival criterion,
ξ ∈H3
nr( e

The relevance of this group to degree-four integral Hodge obstructions is the theorem of
Colliot-Thélène–Voisin [12]. Therefore the local or shadow trajectory contributes to that
obstruction-theoretic target.
□
Remark 7.9. This corollary does not say that every local discriminant class gives an integral
Hodge obstruction. There are several possible failure points. The local class may die under
the support-to-global map. It may fail to produce a degree-three Q/Z residue candidate. It
may produce such a candidate, but one of the residues may be nonzero. Or the resulting
unramified class may be zero. The residue station records only the last of these tests.
Remark 7.10. If the residue maps kill all classes arising from local discriminant data in a fam-
ily, the result is still meaningful. It shows that any integral Hodge obstruction present in that
family is not detected by the local discriminant packages studied here. In the terminology
of this paper, the torsion trajectory dies at the residue station.
7.5. Benoist–Ottem, Coble shadows, and what residue survival can test. The
Benoist–Ottem examples require special care in this framework. On the smooth fiber S ×C,
where S is an Enriques surface, there is no singular local package Ei. The torsion is global
and smooth. The boundary comparison developed in this paper uses the Enriques/Coble
degeneration instead. The relevant boundary singularity is
1

The Benoist–Ottem 2-torsion mechanism selects

through the Bockstein of the local index-two cover

Thus the residue station, if applied to the Benoist–Ottem comparison, should be applied

group E. If such a candidate is produced and all of its residues vanish, then the order-two
Coble shadow contributes to

nr( e
X, Q/Z).
If no such candidate exists, or if a residue is nonzero, then the Benoist–Ottem/Coble trajec-
tory dies before reaching the unramified station.
Remark 7.11. This distinction prevents a common overstatement. The corrected comparison
does not say that the full Coble group

7.6. What this station can and cannot prove. The unramified station should be read
as a test, not as an automatic consequence of local torsion. The formal implication is:
residue candidate ξ with all residues zero

nr.
The formalism does not assert that every local class, or every Bockstein shadow, produces
such a candidate. Producing ξ requires additional geometry.
This distinction is especially important for the examples in the appendices.
(1) For isolated surface singularities, the local group E is well-defined and computable,
but the isolated germ alone does not produce a degree-three unramified class.
(2) For threefold ordinary double points, the local link is torsion-free, so there is no finite
local torsion package to test.
(3) For the Benoist–Ottem benchmark, the smooth fiber has no singular points and
hence no direct local packages Ei. The proposed comparison is degenerational: one
compares global Enriques 2-torsion with the order-two Bockstein shadow
2E ⊂E
inside the Coble boundary package
E ∼= Z/4
for the 1
4(1, 1) singularity.
Thus the residue station is the final obstruction test in the trajectory, but it is not the
first step. Local birth, support transport, global survival, construction of a degree-three
residue candidate, and, in the Coble/Benoist–Ottem setting, selection of the correct order-
two shadow must all occur before the Bloch–Ogus residue criterion can be applied.
8. Patterns extracted from the torsion trajectories
The appendices were constructed as a computational laboratory. Each example was run
through the same stations: local birth, discriminant form, local realizations, support and
pair sequences, global transport status, Brauer and residue status, and rationalization. The
purpose of this section is to record the patterns that emerge from those computations.
The main point is that the examples separate several phenomena that are easy to conflate:
local finite torsion, free vanishing-cycle data, global smooth torsion, Brauer/unramified ob-
struction, and Bockstein-selected shadows inside larger local torsion packages. The examples
show that these are not the same mechanism. The local discriminant package E detects one
very specific phenomenon: finite integral torsion born from the non-unimodularity of local
intersection or linking data. It does not detect every singularity, every node, or every global
torsion class. Moreover, a global torsion mechanism may see only a subgroup or filtered piece
of a local E-package, as happens in the Coble boundary comparison with Benoist–Ottem.
8.1. Local finite torsion is controlled by discriminants. For the normal surface sin-
gularities computed above, the same pattern occurs in every case:
E ∼= H2(L, Z)tors ∼= Λ∨/Λ.
In the hypersurface cases, the same group is also recovered from the monodromy station:
E ∼= coker(T −id)tors.

the theorem in explicit examples.

of the local intersection matrix:
|E| = | det(M)|.
Thus the first pattern is:
local finite torsion is born from non-unimodularity of the local intersection lattice.
Equivalently, the local torsion package is present precisely when the exceptional lattice
has nontrivial discriminant group
Λ∨/Λ.
This explains why E8, despite being a nontrivial singularity with a nontrivial exceptional
configuration, produces no local finite torsion: the E8 lattice is unimodular. It also explains
the Coble boundary package: the local exceptional lattice is [−4], hence
E ∼= coker([−4]) ∼= Z/4.
8.2. E8 is the null-control. The E8 row is important because it prevents a false interpreta-
tion of the invariant. The local group E does not measure the complexity of the singularity in
a naive sense. The E8 singularity has eight exceptional curves and a rich ADE configuration,
but

The link has no torsion in the relevant cohomology group, the pair-sequence quotient van-
ishes, and the monodromy cokernel has trivial torsion. Thus all six local torsion stations
vanish.
The pattern is:
A singularity can be geometrically nontrivial while contributing no local
finite discriminant torsion.
This observation is useful later because it shows that the torsion trajectory is not simply
a singularity detector. It detects a specific integral defect: the failure of the local lattice to
be unimodular.

For Ak,

The pattern is:
the prime decomposition of local torsion is stable across the local realizations.
This does not mean that global transport preserves every prime or every primary exponent.
A prime or exponent may disappear globally if the local class is killed by relations, if only a
Bockstein-selected subgroup is visible, or if the class is later killed by residue conditions. But
the local stations do not create new primes. They present the same finite torsion through
different structures.
8.5. Surface A1 and the threefold node are different phenomena. One of the most

the ordinary double point in complex dimension three.

surface E-type.
The pattern is:
the surface A1 torsion package does not automatically extend to isolated threefold nodes.
This observation is crucial for nodal threefolds. It prevents the incorrect claim that an
ordinary node on a threefold contributes a local Z/2Z torsion class analogous to the surface
A1 group. The threefold node contributes free vanishing-cycle and exceptional curve data,
not local finite discriminant torsion.

The pattern is:
Defect is a global relation phenomenon among free node data, not a local
finite torsion package.
This distinction clarifies the role of nodal quintics in the paper. They are not examples of
local E-torsion. They are contrast examples showing that global relations can be important
even when local finite torsion is absent.

8.7. The natural higher-dimensional carrier of surface torsion is codimension two.
The comparison between surface singularities, threefold nodes, and boundary degeneration
tests suggests a structural principle.
The local group E for a normal surface singularity is a codimension-two phenomenon. A
point on a surface has complex codimension two. The local link is a real three-manifold, and
the obstruction group is
H2(L, Z)tors.
When one passes to higher-dimensional varieties, the natural way to carry the same surface
package is not necessarily through isolated higher-dimensional nodes. Instead, the natural
carrier is a codimension-two stratum with transverse normal surface singularity.
For example, if S0 is a surface with A1 singularities and C is a smooth curve, then
X0 = S0 × C
is a threefold whose singular locus contains curves
Σj = {pj} × C.
Each stratum Σj has transverse singularity A1. The transverse local package is
EA1 ∼= Z/2Z.
Thus the local packages assemble not as isolated point contributions but as finite local
systems along the strata:
Ej ∼= (Z/2Z)Σj.
Similarly, in the corrected Enriques/Coble boundary comparison, the transverse local sin-
gularity is
1
4(1, 1).
The transverse package is
EΣ ∼= (Z/4)Σ.
The Benoist–Ottem-visible part is the order-two sublocal system
2EΣ ∼= (Z/2)Σ.
The pattern is:
The higher-dimensional continuation of the surface package (E, q) is
transverse codimension-two torsion.
This is one of the most important lessons of the examples. It suggests that the right
higher-dimensional theory should not begin with isolated threefold nodes, but with stratified
varieties whose codimension-two strata have surface singularities.

8.10. Death mechanisms in the trajectory. The examples reveal several distinct ways
a torsion trajectory can terminate.
No birth. In the E8 case,
E = 0
because the exceptional lattice is unimodular. No finite torsion is born.
Rational death. In the A1, Ak, D4, Brieskorn, and Coble examples, torsion is born locally
but vanishes after tensoring with Q:
E ⊗Z Q = 0.
This is the ordinary rational death of finite torsion. In the Coble case, both
E ∼= Z/4
and
2E ∼= Z/2
die rationally.

Residue killing. A class that survives to a Brauer or Q/Z-cohomology station may still fail
to be unramified. The Bloch–Ogus residue maps may kill or detect it. Thus residue behavior
is a second global survival test, after the forget-support map.
These mechanisms are distinct. The trajectory keeps them separate.

9. Benoist–Ottem torsion and the Coble boundary E-package
The purpose of this section is to record the corrected local-to-global comparison suggested
by the Enriques/Coble boundary. The natural boundary singularity for the Benoist–Ottem
comparison is not the A1 surface singularity, but the cyclic quotient singularity of type
1
4(1, 1). For this singularity the local perverse obstruction group E from [18] is
E ∼= Z/4.
The Benoist–Ottem 2-torsion mechanism selects the canonical order-two subgroup
2E ∼= Z/2 ⊂E ∼= Z/4.
The relevant Enriques boundary model is supplied by the degree-two Enriques compact-
ification of Alexeev–Engel–Garza–Schaffler. In their description, the discriminant divisor
parametrizes quotients of nodal K3 surfaces by an involution fixing a node; the resulting
boundary surfaces are rational Coble surfaces with a singularity of type 1
4(1, 1) [28]. Thus
the correct local boundary package is the Coble 1
4(1, 1) E-package.

H3(S, Z) ∼= Z/2.
Proposition 9.13 (Bockstein generation of the Benoist–Ottem direction). Let u ∈H2(S, Z/2)

0 →Z
2−→Z →Z/2 →0.
It satisfies the derivation formula
β(a ⌣b) = β(a) ⌣b + (−1)|a|a ⌣β(b).
Apply this to
a = u ∈H2(S, Z/2),

Since |u| = 2, the sign is positive. Thus
βY (u ⊠¯γ) = βS(u) ⊠γ + u ⊠βB(¯γ).

βB(¯γ) = 0.
Therefore
βY (u ⊠¯γ) = βS(u) ⊠γ.
Since βS(u) generates H3(S, Z) ∼= Z/2, these classes generate
H1(B, Z) ⊗H3(S, Z).

10. Trajectory principles, conjectures, and open problems
The trajectory computations suggest a small number of organizing principles. Some are
formal consequences of the computations already carried out; others are conjectural principles
that require new global comparison theorems. We separate these two levels explicitly.
10.1. A proved distinction: surface A1 versus threefold nodes. The first principle is
a proved distinction between two examples that are often informally conflated.
Proposition 10.1 (Surface A1 versus threefold ordinary double point). The A1 surface
singularity has local finite torsion package
E ∼= Z/2Z.
By contrast, the ordinary double point in complex dimension three has link
L ∼= S2 × S3,
and hence
Hm(L, Z)tors = 0
for all m.
Consequently, the threefold ordinary double point has no finite local torsion package of the
surface E-type.
Proof. For the surface A1 singularity, the link is
RP 3.
The computation in Appendix A gives
H2(RP 3, Z)tors ∼= Z/2Z.

The local realization theorem of [18] identifies this group with
E = H0(p
+ICXZ)0.
Hence
E ∼= Z/2Z.
For the ordinary double point in complex dimension three, Appendix F identifies the link
with
S2 × S3.
The integral homology and cohomology of S2 × S3 are torsion-free by the Kunneth theorem.
Therefore
Hm(S2 × S3, Z)tors = 0
for all m. Thus there is no finite local link-torsion group from which a surface-type E-package
could be formed.
□
Remark 10.2. This proposition is one of the main safeguards of the paper. It prevents one
from importing the surface calculation
EA1 ∼= Z/2Z
into the threefold ordinary double point setting. Threefold nodes contribute free vanishing-
cycle and exceptional-curve data, not local finite discriminant torsion.
10.2. A proved null-control: the E8 row. The second principle is that the torsion pack-
age detects non-unimodularity, not merely singularity complexity.
Proposition 10.3 (E8 is the null-control). For the E8 surface singularity, the local finite
torsion package vanishes:
E = 0.
Equivalently,
H2(L, Z)tors = 0,
Λ∨/Λ = 0,
coker(T −id)tors = 0.
Proof. The exceptional lattice of the E8 singularity is the negative definite E8 lattice. The
E8 lattice is unimodular, so the natural map
Λ −→Λ∨
is an isomorphism. Hence
Λ∨/Λ = 0.
By the local realization theorem of [18],
E ∼= Λ∨/Λ.
Therefore E = 0. The link and monodromy formulations are the corresponding realizations
of the same local group, computed explicitly in Appendix D.
□
Remark 10.4. Thus a singularity may have a nontrivial exceptional configuration while con-
tributing no finite local discriminant torsion. The invariant E detects the discriminant of
the local lattice, not singularity complexity itself.

10.3. Form-sensitive survival. The D4 computation shows that one must track a finite
pairing, not only a finite group.
Conjecture 10.5 (Form-sensitive survival). The global survival of a local finite torsion class
depends on the local discriminant package
(E, q),
not only on the abstract finite abelian group E.
Remark 10.6. The D4 example motivates this conjecture. It has underlying group
E ∼= (Z/2Z)2,
but the discriminant form is nontrivial and contains off-diagonal Q/Z-valued information.
Two singularity configurations may have isomorphic underlying torsion groups but inequiva-
lent discriminant forms. A global transport theory that forgets the form may therefore miss
information visible in the link pairing and in the exceptional lattice.
Remark 10.7. The Coble boundary example adds a second form-sensitive lesson. For the
singularity 1
4(1, 1), the full package is
E ∼= Z/4,
q(¯g, ¯g) = −1
4.
The Benoist–Ottem mechanism detects the order-two subgroup
2E ∼= Z/2
rather than the full group. Thus a global obstruction theory may be sensitive not only to
the finite group and its form, but also to a functorially selected subgroup or quotient of the
local package.
10.4. The codimension-two transverse torsion principle. The examples suggest that
the natural higher-dimensional continuation of the surface package E is not an isolated
higher-dimensional ordinary double point. Rather, it is a codimension-two stratum with
transverse normal surface singularity.
Conjecture 10.8 (Codimension-two transverse torsion principle). Let X be a complex variety
with a codimension-two stratum
Σ ⊂X
whose transverse singularity is a normal surface singularity with local discriminant package
(E, q).
Then the higher-dimensional carrier of this torsion is a finite local system
E
on Σ, with fiber E. The global contribution of the transverse torsion package is governed by
the cohomology groups
Hr(Σ, E),
together with their support, Brauer, residue, and rationalization trajectories.
Remark 10.9. This conjecture is motivated by the comparison between threefold ordinary
double points and products S0 × C, where S0 has surface singularities. Isolated threefold
nodes have torsion-free link S2×S3. By contrast, a codimension-two stratum with transverse
normal surface singularity carries the surface package E along the stratum.

10.5. A product test for the codimension-two principle. The codimension-two trans-
verse torsion principle is conjectural in general, but it has a basic product test case. This
test case is not the corrected Enriques/Coble boundary model; rather, it is the clean local A1
model that illustrates how a surface package becomes a local system along a codimension-two
stratum.
Proposition 10.10 (Product test for transverse A1-torsion). Let (S0, p) be a normal surface
germ with an A1 singularity at p, and let C be a smooth connected projective curve. Set
X0 := S0 × C,
Σ := {p} × C.
Then Σ is a codimension-two singular stratum of X0, and the transverse singularity along Σ
is A1. The transverse local discriminant package has fiber
EA1 ∼= Z/2Z.
Moreover, because the product structure has constant transverse analytic type along Σ, these
fibers assemble into the constant finite local system
EΣ ∼= (Z/2Z)Σ.
Consequently,
Hr(Σ, EΣ) ∼= Hr(C, Z/2Z)
for all r. In particular, if C has genus g, then
H1(Σ, EΣ) ∼= (Z/2Z)2g.
Proof. Since S0 is a surface and C is a smooth curve, the product X0 = S0 ×C is a threefold.
The singular locus of X0 near {p} × C is
Sing(X0) = Sing(S0) × C.
After shrinking S0 around p, the surface S0 has no singular point other than p. Hence the
singular locus of X0 is exactly
Σ = {p} × C.
Since X0 has complex dimension 3 and Σ has complex dimension 1, the stratum Σ has
codimension 2.
Let c ∈C. A neighborhood of (p, c) ∈X0 is analytically a product of a neighborhood of
p ∈S0 with a disk in C. Therefore a transverse slice to Σ at (p, c) is analytically isomorphic
to the surface germ (S0, p). By hypothesis this transverse germ is the A1 surface singularity.
The A1 trajectory computation gives
EA1 ∼= Z/2Z.
Equivalently,
EA1 ∼= H2(RP 3, Z)tors ∼= Λ∨/Λ ∼= Z/2Z,
where Λ = [−2] is the exceptional lattice of the minimal resolution of the A1 singularity.
This is the computation carried out in Appendix A and follows from the local realization
theorem recalled in Section 4.
Because the local analytic model is the product (S0, p)×C, the transverse singularity does
not vary as c moves along C. Thus the fiber EA1 is identified canonically along the stratum.
The resulting finite local system on Σ is therefore constant:
EΣ ∼= (Z/2Z)Σ.

ABDUL RAHMAN
Since Σ ∼= C, we obtain
Hr(Σ, EΣ) ∼= Hr(C, Z/2Z).
Finally, if C has genus g, its first homology group is
H1(C, Z) ∼= Z2g.
By the universal coefficient theorem with coefficients Z/2Z,
H1(C, Z/2Z) ∼= Hom(H1(C, Z), Z/2Z) ∼= (Z/2Z)2g.
This proves the claim.
□
Remark 10.11 (The curve case). The product test used in the Benoist–Ottem/Coble com-
parison is the special case
B = C,
where C is a smooth projective curve. In that case
Σ = {p} × C ∼= C,
and for the Coble boundary package one obtains
H1(Σ, 2EΣ) ∼= H1(C, Z/2).
If C has genus g, then
H1(C, Z/2) ∼= (Z/2)2g.
Equivalently, these classes classify the degree-two local systems, or unramified double covers,

10.8. Nodal defect as free-relation data. The nodal examples also suggest a clean sep-
aration between finite torsion and free relation phenomena.
Proposition 10.17 (Nodal defect is not local finite torsion). Ordinary double points on
projective threefolds contribute free local vanishing-cycle or exceptional-curve data, not finite
local discriminant torsion. Consequently, nodal defect should be interpreted as a global rela-
tion phenomenon among free node classes, not as the kernel of a direct local finite torsion
map.
Proof. For each ordinary double point in complex dimension three, the link is
S2 × S3.
As proved above, this link has torsion-free integral cohomology. Therefore each node has no
local finite torsion package of the surface E-type.
On the other hand, the Milnor fiber of a threefold ordinary double point has the homotopy
type of S3, so each node has free vanishing-cycle data. In a small resolution, the node is
replaced by an exceptional P1, giving free exceptional-curve data. Relations among these
free local classes are global relations. This is the type of relation measured by defect in the


## Page 92


92
ABDUL RAHMAN
nodal hypersurface literature [25, 26, 27]. Hence nodal defect is not the kernel of a direct
finite local torsion map.
□

10.11. Outlook. The computations in this paper suggest that the next stage of the program
should focus on codimension-two strata with transverse surface singularities. The required
theory should combine:
(1) the local discriminant package (E, q) for normal surface singularities;
(2) the formation of finite local systems of such packages along codimension-two strata;
(3) support and excision maps for these local systems;
(4) comparison with Brauer and unramified cohomology;
(5) Bockstein-selected subpackages such as 2E ⊂E;
(6) motivic lifts of the form
i∗(1Σ/n)[2];
(7) specialization maps in degenerating families.

The main open problem is to determine when such transverse local torsion packages, or
their Bockstein-selected shadows, survive globally and when they account for known integral
Hodge obstructions.
Appendix A. The A1 surface trajectory
This appendix works out the torsion trajectory for the A1 surface singularity in full de-
tail. This is the basic local model for Z/2-torsion in the paper. The computation is useful
because all six local realizations of the obstruction group can be written explicitly: perverse
discrepancy, torsion-sensitive truncation, link torsion, linking form, resolution lattice, pair
sequence, and monodromy. The conclusion is that the same group
E ∼= Z/2Z
appears in each realization, with discriminant form
q(1, 1) = −1
2 mod Z
under the negative definite geometric intersection convention fixed in the main text.
This example should be distinguished from the Enriques/Coble boundary example. The
A1 package is the clean local Z/2-model and it appears as the index-two cover of the Coble
boundary singularity
A1 = C2/µ2 −→C2/µ4 = 1
4(1, 1).
On links this is
L(2, 1) −→L(4, 1).
Thus A1 remains essential to the corrected Benoist–Ottem comparison, but the boundary
singularity itself is 1
4(1, 1), whose full local package is Z/4.
A.1. Local model. The A1 surface singularity is the hypersurface germ
(X, 0) = {x2 + y2 + z2 = 0} ⊂(C3, 0).
Equivalently, after a linear change of coordinates, it is analytically isomorphic to the quadric
cone
{uv −w2 = 0} ⊂C3.
It is also the quotient singularity
C2/{±1},
where −1 acts by
(a, b) 7−→(−a, −b).
The quotient map can be written explicitly as
C2 −→{uv −w2 = 0},
(a, b) 7−→(u, v, w) = (a2, b2, ab).
Indeed,
uv −w2 = a2b2 −a2b2 = 0,
and the functions a2, b2, ab generate the invariant ring

A.10. Transport status. The A1 computation above is a local surface computation. It
determines the birth, form, and six local realizations of the torsion group
E ∼= Z/2Z.
However, it does not by itself define a global Brauer or unramified class. To obtain a global
class, one must place the singularity inside a global variety and specify the relevant local-to-
global support map.
For the local resolution neighborhood N of the exceptional curve C, cohomology with
supports along C is related to ordinary cohomology by the long exact sequence
· · · −→H2
C(N, Z) −→H2(N, Z) −→H2(N \ C, Z) −→H3
C(N, Z) −→· · · .
The space N \ C deformation retracts onto the boundary circle bundle L = ∂N, so
H2(N \ C, Z) ∼= H2(L, Z) ∼= Z/2Z.
The Thom isomorphism for the oriented real rank-two normal bundle of C in N gives
H2
C(N, Z) ∼= H0(C, Z) ∼= Z,
and
H3
C(N, Z) ∼= H1(C, Z) = 0.
Thus the relevant portion of the support sequence becomes
Z −→Z −→Z/2Z −→0.
The first map is multiplication by the Euler class of the normal bundle, which is the self-
intersection
C2 = −2.
Therefore the cokernel is
Z/2Z.
This is another way to see that the local torsion is a boundary quotient created by the
exceptional divisor.
Thus, for an isolated A1 singularity on a global surface, the local torsion naturally appears
in the degree-two local support sequence around the exceptional curve. It is not automatically
a degree-three class. The degree-three Brauer channel used for smooth projective threefolds
therefore requires a separate global or higher-dimensional construction.
In trajectory language, the local A1 surface row has the following transport status:
support degree: 2 locally, through the exceptional curve support sequence.
A global image depends on how the exceptional curve class maps into the global cohomology
of the chosen resolution. If several singularities are present, global relations among excep-
tional curve classes may kill linear combinations of the local torsion packages. This is the
surface analogue of the kernel phenomenon studied in the main text.

Appendix B. The Ak surface trajectory
This appendix works out the torsion trajectory for the Ak surface singularities. The A1
case is the basic 2-torsion atom, while the Ak family shows how the same surface mechanism
produces arbitrary cyclic torsion:
E ∼= Z/(k + 1)Z.
The computation also fixes the generator convention for the discriminant form of the negative
definite Ak exceptional lattice.

B.7. Pair-sequence realization. Let N be a resolution neighborhood of the exceptional
chain
C1 ∪· · · ∪Ck.
Then N deformation retracts onto the exceptional divisor. The boundary of N is the link
L = ∂N.
The long exact sequence of the pair (N, L) contains
H2(L, Z) −→H2(N, Z) −→H2(N, L; Z) −→H1(L, Z) −→H1(N, Z).
For the Ak resolution neighborhood,
H2(N, Z) ∼= Zk,
generated by the exceptional curves C1, . . . , Ck. The neighborhood N is obtained by plumb-
ing disk bundles over spheres according to a tree, so
H1(N, Z) = 0.
Also H2(L, Z) = 0 for the lens space L = L(k + 1, k). Therefore the relevant part of the
exact sequence becomes
0 −→Zk −→H2(N, L; Z) −→H1(L, Z) −→0.
By Poincare–Lefschetz duality for the compact oriented real four-manifold N,
H2(N, L; Z) ∼= H2(N, Z).
Since N deformation retracts onto the exceptional chain, and the exceptional chain has
H2 ∼= Zk and no H1, the universal coefficient theorem gives
H2(N, Z) ∼= Hom(H2(N, Z), Z) ∼= Zk.
Under these identifications, the map
H2(N, Z) −→H2(N, L; Z)
is the intersection pairing, hence is represented by the matrix M. Therefore
H1(L, Z) ∼= coker(M) ∼= Z/(k + 1)Z.
By the universal coefficient theorem for the closed oriented three-manifold L,
H2(L, Z)tors ∼= H1(L, Z)tors ∼= Z/(k + 1)Z.
Thus the pair sequence recovers
E ∼= Z/(k + 1)Z.
This is the Ak specialization of the general pair-sequence computation in [18, §4], and it is
compatible with the linking-pairing framework of [6].

To see that the cokernel is cyclic, observe that T −I has a (k −1) × (k −1) minor of
determinant ±1: the upper-left (k −1) × (k −1) block is lower triangular with diagonal
entries −1. Hence the first k −1 Smith invariant factors are equal to 1. Since the product
of all Smith invariant factors is
| det(T −I)| = k + 1,
the final Smith invariant factor is k + 1. Therefore
coker(T −id) ∼= Z/(k + 1)Z.

E ∼= coker(T −id)tors ∼= Z/(k + 1)Z.

|E| = k + 1 = | det(T −id)|.
B.9. Transport status. The Ak computation above is a local surface computation.
It
determines the birth, form, and six local realizations of the torsion group
E ∼= Z/(k + 1)Z.
As in the A1 case, this local surface package naturally appears through the support and pair
sequences associated to the exceptional curve chain.
Let N be a resolution neighborhood of the exceptional divisor. The support sequence for
the exceptional divisor and the pair sequence for (N, L) identify the local boundary quotient
with
coker(M) ∼= Z/(k + 1)Z.
Thus the local surface package is naturally associated to the degree-two local topology of
the exceptional curve configuration. It is not, by itself, a degree-three Brauer class.
If an Ak singularity occurs inside a global surface, the global image of its torsion package
depends on how the exceptional chain maps into the global lattice of exceptional and divisor
classes. If several singularities are present, global relations among exceptional chains may
kill linear combinations of the local torsion packages. Thus the transport status of the Ak
surface row is:
support degree: 2 locally, through the exceptional-chain support/pair sequence.
To enter the degree-three Brauer channel, the Ak package must be placed inside a suitable
global threefold mechanism or compared with a higher-dimensional local package whose
torsion appears in degree 3.
